Duality for p p -adic geometric pro-étale cohomology Thanks: P.C. and W.N.’s research was supported in part by the grant NR-19-CE40-0015-02 COLOSS and by the Simons Foundation. S.G.’s research was partially supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-1926686.
Pierre Colmez, Sally Gilles, Wiesława Nizioł

Abstract
. We prove that p p -adic geometric pro-étale cohomology of smooth partially proper rigid analytic varieties over p p -adic fields seen in the category of Topological Vector Spaces satisfies a Poincaré duality as we have conjectured. This duality descends, via fully-faithfulness results of Colmez-Nizioł, from a Poincaré duality for solid quasi-coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve representing this cohomology. The latter duality is proved by passing, via comparison theorems, to analogous sheaves representing syntomic cohomology and then reducing to Poincaré duality for 𝐁 st + {\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}} -twisted Hyodo-Kato and filtered 𝐁 dR + {\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}} -cohomologies that, in turn, reduce to Serre duality for smooth Stein varieties – a classical result.



1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a prime and let
K
be a discrete
p
-adic field
with perfect residue field.
Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
and let
C=\widehat{\overline{K}}
be the
p
-adic completion of
\overline{K}
.

1.1.
Duality Conjecture

In
[
15
,
16
]
, the authors showed that (a part of) the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence for
{\rm GL}_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
can be realized in the
p
-adic (pro-)étale cohomology of the Drinfeld
tower in dimension
1
. It involved the duals of the unitary representations of
{\rm GL}_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})

provided by the
p
-adic local Langlands correspondence and not the representations themselves.
In contrast,
the classical local Langlands correspondence (not its dual)
is realized in the compactly supported
\ell
-adic cohomology rather than the usual one;
this brought us to consider a possibility of duality for
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology.

The reason the authors of loc. cit.
worked with the usual cohomology was mainly due to their newly acquired competence
with comparison theorems for analytic varieties
[
20
]
.
In retrospect, this was a lucky choice since the description of
geometric pro-étale cohomology with compact support is not as transparent as that
of usual cohomology. The compactly supported cohomology (even in dimension
1
)
was not defined at that time, but we did some heuristic computations
assuming that it could be defined similarly
to de Rham cohomology with compact support
[
33
]
, and that (derived)
comparison theorems would work for it as expected.
The first results did not look very promising.

Here is the simplest of them.
Let
D
be the open disc of dimension
1
over
K
. Using syntomic methods,
we obtain that the only nontrivial cohomology groups are as follows:

\displaystyle H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}(1),\quad H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(D_{C})/C,

(1.1)

\displaystyle H^{2}_{{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq{\bf Q}_{p}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}(\partial D_{C})/{\mathcal{O}}(D_{C}),

where
\partial D_{C}
denotes the "boundary of
D_{C}
". Since we have the isomorphisms
(of topological
C
-vector spaces)

{\mathcal{O}}(D_{C})/C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Omega^{1}(D_{C}),\quad{\mathcal{O}}(\partial D_{C})/{\mathcal{O}}(D_{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{1}_{c}(D_{C},{\mathcal{O}}),

we see in (
1.1
) a Serre duality (of topological
C
-vector spaces)

\Omega^{1}(D_{C})\simeq H^{1}_{c}(D_{C},{\mathcal{O}})^{*}

as well as a simple
{\bf Q}_{p}
-duality (between
H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))
and the
{\bf Q}_{p}
appearing in

H^{2}_{{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},c}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))
) but they do not fit together into a simple
Poincaré duality (since the degrees do not match, and the
C
-duality cannot be turned into
a
{\bf Q}_{p}
-duality as
[C:{\bf Q}_{p}]=\infty
).

However, we realized (after quite a while) that, if we couple the above with the following
computations
1
1

1

In (
1.2
)
the last nontrivial
\operatorname{Ext}
group is generated by the fundamental exact sequence

0\to{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1)\to{\mathbb{B}}^{+,\varphi=p}_{\operatorname{cr}}\to{\mathbb{G}}_{a}\to 0,

where
{\mathbb{B}}^{+,\varphi=p}_{\operatorname{cr}}
is the TVS corresponding to
{{\bf B}}^{+,\varphi=p}_{\operatorname{cr}}
.

in the category of Banach-Colmez spaces (
\mathrm{BC}
’s for short) and
assume that the results remain valid
in the bigger category of Topological Vector Spaces (
{\rm TVS}
’s for short):

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{\rm TVS}({{\bf Q}}_{p},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1),\quad\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({{\bf Q}}_{p},{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1))=0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{G}}_{a},{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1))=0,\quad\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{G}}_{a},{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1))\simeq{\mathbb{G}}_{a},

\displaystyle\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}_{\rm TVS}(M,N)=0,\quad i\geq 2,\ M,N\in\mathrm{BC},

as well as that
{\rm Ext}
-groups of tensor products with constant objects behave as if
everything was finite dimensional, we get abstract isomorphisms
(with
H^{i}_{?}:=H^{i}_{?}(D_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))
)

H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}\simeq{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}(H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c},{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1))\quad H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}(H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c},{{\bf Q}}_{p}(1))

(1.3)

and an abstract exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\rm TVS}(H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to H^{2}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}\to{\rm Hom}_{\rm TVS}(H^{0}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\to 0,

(1.4)

which suggest a (derived) duality, both ways.

These examples brought us to formulate the following conjecture (see
[
13
]
,
[
17
]
):

Conjecture 1.5
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid analytic variety over
K
of dimension
d
.
In the category of Topological Vector Spaces we have a natural quasi-isomorphism

{\mathbb{R}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{R}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d))[2d],{\bf Q}_{p}).

Here:

(1)

TVS’s are
\underline{{\bf Q}}_{p}
-modules in the category of topologically enriched
2
2

2

For the sake of the introduction we invite the reader to ignore the issue of enrichment and think of TVS’s as just topological presheaves. See
[
19
]
for precise definitions.
presheaves on strictly totally disconnected spaces over
C
,
denoted
{\rm sPerf}_{C}
, with values in solid abelian groups.

(2)

{\mathbb{R}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
is a TVS defined by
S\mapsto{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})
; the topology on
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})
is canonically inherited from the pro-étale site.

(3)

Pro-étale cohomology with compact support
{\mathbb{R}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
is defined by
S\mapsto{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})
, where,
for an exhaustive covering
\{U_{n}\}
,
U_{n}\Subset U_{n+1}
, by quasi-compact open spaces, we set

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle:=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})],

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X_{S},{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{S}\mathbin{\fgebackslash}U_{n,S},{\bf Q}_{p}),

with the induced topology.
By
[
1
]
, we have
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
, the Huber
p
-adic étale cohomology.

Remark 1.6
.

We state the duality only one way because of potential pathologies for extensions of Fréchet
(or Banach) spaces in the condensed world, but (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) suggest
strongly that there should be
a duality both ways, at least in reasonnable cases.

1.2.
The main result

The main result of this paper is the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

Conjecture
1.5
holds.

Our strategy for the proof of Theorem
1.7
follows the heuristic computations we have done on examples.
The foundational results needed to do that mentioned in the previous section
were proven in a series of papers by the authors and Piotr Achinger: Hyodo-Kato cohomology of rigid analytic and dagger varieties was defined and studied in
[
21
]
,
p
-adic comparison theorems were proven in
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
,
[
25
]
, compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology and Hyodo-Kato dualities were studied in
[
1
]
, the properties of Topological Vector Spaces were studied in
[
19
]
(that they satisfy the expected duality for BC’s was derived there from a result of the same type due to Anschütz-Le Bras in the category of Vector Spaces
3
3

3

That is
\infty
-derived category of
\underline{{\bf Q}}_{p}
-modules on the site of perfectoid affinoids over
C
equipped with pro-étale topology.

[
4
]
).

To prove Theorem
1.7
, we start with passing from pro-étale cohomology to syntomic cohomology.
Recall that the latter is built from the Hyodo-Kato part, that records the mod
p
information, and the de filtered Rham part that records the characteristic zero information; the two parts are connected via the Hyodo-Kato morphism.
It is part of the standard yoga of
p
-adic Hodge theory for algebraic varieties that, when dealing with syntomic cohomology one should work as long as possible separately with the Hyodo-Kato and the de Rham parts and glue them together only at the last moment.
4
4

4

A technique inherited from Beilinson-Deligne cohomology (see
[
7
]
). For how this works for syntomic cohomology in the algebraic setting see
[
6
]
,
[
23
]
.
In the perfectoid world this separation can be obtained geometrically by representing syntomic cohomology by a quasi-coherent sheaf on the Fargues-Fontaine curve.
This sheaf will have (completed) stalks equal to twisted Hyodo-Kato cohomology at all points outside of
\infty
and at
\infty
it will be equal to the filtered
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology.
Now Hyodo-Kato duality (inherited itself from de Rham duality) and filtered de Rham duality (see
[
1
]
) yield a duality on the Fargues-Fontaine curve. Taking derived global sections of this duality, via fully-faithfulness results from
[
19
]
, yields a duality on the level of TVS’s.

1.3.
A Corollary

Before reviewing our proof of Theorem
1.7
we will state an implication and sketch its proof. It contains many of the essential elements of the proof of the main theorem.

Corollary 1.8
.

Let
i\geq 0
. There is a natural short exact sequence of
TVS
’s

0\to{\mathcal{E}xt}^{1}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{H}}^{2d-i+1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d)),{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathcal{H}om}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{H}}^{2d-i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d)),{\bf Q}_{p})\to 0

We note that the term on right is almost constant (see the computations (
1.2
)).
Since we have the internal
{\mathrm{R}}{\rm{Hom}}
spectral sequence, it suffices to show that

{\mathcal{E}xt}^{a}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{H}}^{b}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\bf Q}_{p})=0,\quad a\geq 2.

(1.9)

Using syntomic comparison theorems we can trivialize the TVS-structure on pro-étale cohomology. That is, for
r\geq 2d
, we have a long exact sequence of TVS’s

\cdots\to\mathbb{DR}^{b-1}_{c}(X_{C},r)\to{\mathbb{H}}^{b}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to\mathbb{HK}^{b}_{c}(X_{C},r)\to\mathbb{DR}^{b}_{c}(X_{C},r)\to\cdots,

where we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{HK}^{b}_{c}(X_{C},r)

\displaystyle:=(H^{b}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X_{C})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\mathbb{B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{cr}})^{\varphi=p^{r}}

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{DR}^{b}_{c}(X_{C},r)

\displaystyle:=\lim(\cdots\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{j})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{K}({\mathbb{B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r})\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{j+1})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{K}({\mathbb{B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r-1})\to\cdots)[-d]

Here
\breve{C}={\rm Frac}(W(\overline{k}))
, where
k
is the residue field of
K
.
We note that the TVS’s structure in (
1.10
) comes solely from the period presheaves.

To show (
1.9
), modulo a boundary case, it suffices to show it for
the Hyodo-Kato and de Rham parts separately.
For the Hyodo-Kato part, passing via a limit argument to overconvergent quasi-compact opens, we may assume that the ranks of Hyodo-Kato groups are finite.
But then
(H^{b}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X_{C})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\mathbb{B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{cr}})^{\varphi=p^{r}}
is a BC and we know that the
\operatorname{Ext}
-groups for those vanish in degrees higher than 2.
So far there was no functional analytic difficulties. They appear when we need to show that

{\mathcal{E}xt}^{a}_{\rm TVS}(H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{i})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}{\mathbb{G}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{p})=0,\quad a\geq 2.

But now the space
H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{i})
is of compact type thus, assuming
K={\bf Q}_{p}
for simplicity, we have

{\mathcal{E}xt}^{a}_{\rm TVS}(H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{i})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}{\mathbb{G}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{i})^{*}\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Q}_{p}}{\mathcal{E}xt}^{a}_{\rm TVS}({\mathbb{G}}_{a},{\bf Q}_{p}),

which vanishes in the required range (by the BC computations), as wanted.

1.4.
Duality for
p
-adic geometric pro-étale cohomology on the Fargues-Fontaine curve

We show in this paper that the
p
-adic geometric pro-étale cohomology seen as living on the Fargues-Fontaine curve satisfies a Poincaré duality. Recall that the
p
-adic geometric pro-étale cohomology of a smooth partially proper rigid analytic variety
X
over
K
can be represented
by a solid quasi-coherent sheaf on the Fargues-Fontaine curve, i.e.,
the pro-étale cohomology can be computed as

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{FF}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})),

for a (nuclear) solid quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
on the Fargues-Fontaine
curve
X_{\operatorname{FF}}:=X_{\operatorname{FF},C^{\flat}}
defined using relative period sheaves.
Similarly, geometric compactly supported pro-étale cohomology

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
can be represented by solid quasi-coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})
on
X_{\operatorname{FF}}
. See Section
4.1.2
for the definitions.

Via comparison theorems, we see that, if
?\in\{-,c\}
,

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\simeq{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{syn},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),\quad r\geq 2d,

where
d
is the dimension of
X
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{syn},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))

is the syntomic cohomology sheaf (a solid quasi-coherent sheaf on the Fargues-Fontaine curve representing syntomic cohomology; see Section
3.3
for a definition).
This is equivalent to proving a comparison theorem between corresponding Frobenius equivariant sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
, which amounts to untwisting Frobenius from classical comparison theorems.
Luckily for us, the proofs of comparison theorems in
[
21
]
and
[
1
]
do actually (implicitly) prove the untwisted versions we want (see Corollary
4.9
for the notation):

Theorem 1.11
.

(
{\mathbb{B}}^{I}
-comparison theorem)

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper variety over
K
. Let
r\geq 0
and
let
I\subset(0,\infty)
be a compact interval with rational endpoints
5
5

5

Containing the fixed intervals from Section
2.2.2
. We have a natural, functorial in
S
, compatible with Frobenius,
quasi-isomorphism
in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{I}_{S^{\flat},\Box})
:

\displaystyle\tau_{\leq r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},?}(X_{S},{\mathbb{B}}^{I})(r)

\displaystyle\simeq\tau_{\leq r}[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{I}}_{\operatorname{HK},?}(X_{S},r)\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\iota^{I}_{\operatorname{HK}}}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}^{I}(X_{S},r)].

Recall that classical syntomic cohomology is built from
(\varphi,N)
-eigenspaces of
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}
-twisted Hyodo-Kato cohomology and from filtered
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology. Representing it (in a stable range) by the sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{syn},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
on the Fargues-Fontaine curve separates these terms: heuristically speaking, the (completed) stalks of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{syn},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
at points outside
\infty
are
N
-eigenspaces of
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}
-twisted Hyodo-Kato cohomology and the (completed) stalk at
\infty
is the
r
-th filtration level of

{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology.

Now, the stalk cohomology sheaves satisfy Poincaré duality: Poincaré duality for Hyodo-Kato cohomology reduces, via the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism, to that for de Rham cohomology and Poincaré duality for filtered de Rham cohomology, in turn, reduces to Serre duality for smooth Stein varieties – a classical result (see
[
1
]
and Section
5.1
for details).
These dualities are inherited by the sheaves
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{syn},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
, for
r\geq 2d
, and then by the sheaves
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))
yielding the second main result of this paper:

Theorem 1.12
.

(Poincaré duality for pro-étale sheaves)

We have a natural, Galois equivariant, quasi-isomorphim in
{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF}})

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF}})}({\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(d))[2d],{\mathcal{O}}).

(1.13)

The proof of the theorem does not proceed as sketched above though,
due to the difficulties of passing to stalks in the theory of solid quasi-coherent sheaves.
Instead we argue in a similar vein with
\varphi
-modules on the
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
-curve.
In a side remark,
we sketch an alternative proof of Theorem
1.12

that, instead of passing to the
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
-curve, uses dual modifications.

Analogous argument, with splitting into Hyodo-Kato and de Rham terms, yields a Künneth formula:

Theorem 1.14
.

(Künneth formula)

Let
X,Y
be smooth partially proper varieties over
K
.
Then the canonical map

\kappa:\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}((X\times_{K}Y)_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})

is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF}})
.

Remark 1.15
.

In Theorem
1.12
and Theorem
1.14
, we can replace
C
, functorially, with any affinoid perfectoid over
C
.

1.5.
Descend to TVS’s

Finally, to prove Theorem
1.7
, we need to descend the duality (
1.13
) to the "real" world, which for us is the world of Topological Vector Spaces.
We apply the projection functor

{\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}:{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF}})\to{\rm TVS},

the derived global section functor from
[
19
]
, to the duality on the Fargues-Fontaine curve (
1.13
) and, since

{\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathbb{R}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},?}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),\quad{\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}{\mathcal{O}}\simeq{\bf Q}_{p},

we reduce to showing that the canonical map

{\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}{\mathrm{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\rm QCoh(X_{\operatorname{FF}})}({\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}),{\mathcal{O}}){\to}{\mathrm{R}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\rm TVS}({\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c},{\mathrm{R}}\tau_{*}{\mathcal{O}}).

is a quasi-isomorphism.
But this fully-faithfulness result can be reduced by an argument similar to the one used in the proof of the Corollary
1.8
to fully-faithfulness for perfect complexes on the Fargues-Fontaine curve and this was proven in
[
19
]
.

Remark 1.16
.

(1)(
Algebraic Poincaré Duality
) The duality in Conjecture (
1.5
) has an algebraic version in the category of Vector Spaces (see Corollary
6.19
for the statement). It is deduced from Theorem
1.7
via fully-faithfulness results from
[
19
]
.

(2) (
Arithmetic Duality
) Our Conjecture
1.5
has an arithmetic version, i.e., for

p
-adic arithmetic pro-étale cohomology (see
[
13
]
,
[
17
]
). The statement is much simpler: it is a Poincaré duality in the category of topological spaces that yields a nonderived version working in "both directions".
In
[
17
, Th. 1.1]
, we have proved this conjecture for dagger curves over
K
via relatively down to earth techniques (
p
-adic comparison theorems, Serre duality, reciprocity laws via
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules). The general case was derived from Theorem
1.12
via Galois descent by Zhenghui Li in
[
28
]
.

1.6.
The story of this paper and related work

The proof of duality presented in this paper is quite simple.
But a lot of foundational work went into setting up the right formalism for this to be the case.
Here is the story how this developed.

We started working on this project in the spring of 2020 when the computations for the open ball suggested
that there could be a Poincaré duality for
p
-adic geometric pro-étale cohomology
of rigid analytic spaces provided one could mix
the degrees of cohomology and combine
{\bf Q}_{p}
-duality and
C
-duality.
This suggested that there could
be a duality in the
\mathrm{BC}
-category, by using
{\rm RHom}(-,{\bf Q}_{p})
instead of
{\rm Hom}(-,{\bf Q}_{p})
; for this to
work one needed the vanishing of
{\rm Ext}^{i}
’s for
i\geq 2
in the
\mathrm{BC}
-category (and in fact in
a bigger category containing the TVS’s appearing in our comparison theorems with syntomic cohomology).
We discussed this with Fargues and Le Bras in Oberwolfach at the first post-covid conference in
July 2020.
The required vanishing in the VS-category were proved the following year by Anschütz and Le Bras
[
4
]

by a reduction to a theorem of Breen
(we realized, more recently, that these vanishings are elementary in the
\mathrm{BC}
-category itself).
We needed a version of this result for TVS’s but it was a strong indication that what we wanted could be true.

We were at the time in the middle of writing
[
21
,
22
]
which contained part
of the foundational tools needed for a Poincaré duality
(definition of geometric Hyodo-Kato cohomology, geometrization
of
p
-adic comparison theorems for usual cohomology), and
we started considering their compact support avatars
[
1
]
.
Concurrently, we did some extra computations for analytifications of
algebraic curves, which again pointed strongly towards the existence of a Poincaré duality
in the geometric and arithmetic cases (the computations were more involved in the arithmetic case,
but the groups that were appearing looked much more manageable, belonging to the usual
world of
{\bf Q}_{p}
-topological vector spaces).
By the time of the workshop "Non-Archimedean Geometry and Applications" of February 2022, in Oberwolfach, we had a conjecture
[
13
]
and a strategy
that seemed to work well in examples, starting from our geometrized comparison theorem, and reinterpreting
syntomic cohomology as quasi-coherent
\varphi
-equivariant cohomology on the Fargues-Fontaine
Y
-curve.
We presented these results at the workshop and were quite excited to discover that there were
two other talks dealing with Poincaré
duality: one by Zavyalov establishing
[
34
]

Poincaré duality for proper analytic varieties over
C
, and
one by Mann, developing
[
29
]
a 6-functors formalism for
{\mathcal{O}}^{+}/p
-local systems from which he
could also deduce Poincaré duality for proper analytic varieties over
C
(note that, for
proper varieties, the pro-étale cohomology groups – for constant coefficients –
are finite dimensional
{\bf Q}_{p}
-vector spaces, and there
is no need to consider
\mathrm{BC}
-duality).

Since arithmetic duality only involved familiar objects, we decided that it would be wise to start
by the proof of our conjecture for arithmetic duality for curves
[
17
]
, but in the end we had to use
the condensed formalism to handle functional analytic questions arising in topological dualities.
For the geometric duality almost all the tools we needed were at hand: luckily for us, Andreychev
[
2
]
developed the theory of solid quasi-coherent sheaves that created a framework in which to express the duality on the Fargues-Fontaine curve that we envisaged.
That was sufficient to prove a duality on the Fargues-Fontaine curve.
What was missing to carry out our strategy in full was a condensed version of TVS’s from
[
22
]

and the
\operatorname{Ext}
-vanishing we mentioned above.
That took us longer than expected mostly because we have experimented with various possible definitions settling in the end on almost verbatim translation.
And, finally, in
[
19
]
, we deduced the vanishing in TVS’s from the one in VS’s via a fully-faithfulness result.
In the meantime, Zhenghui Li
[
28
]
proved in his thesis, much to our surprise
6
6

6

We were rather expecting a Galois descent from TVS’s.
, that
the duality at the level of the Fargues-Fontaine curve was enough to deduce the existence
of an arithmetic duality in general, which prompted us to put out
[
18
]
, a preliminary version of this paper.

1.6.1.
Related work

In a related work, Anschütz, Le Bras, and Mann (see
[
5
]
) followed a different path towards the
proof of Conjecture
1.5
in the VS-form (see Remark
1.16
).
They also proceed in two steps: the first step is a duality on the Fargues-Fontaine curve, which is a byproduct of the 6-functor formalism for solid quasi-coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve they have developed; the second step is a descend to the world of VS’s.
Their first step is very different in nature and techniques from ours and includes coefficients.
On the other hand, the second step, while it passes through solid sheaves of Fargues-Scholze instead
of our TVS’s, is very similar to ours: it relies on the Hyodo-Kato comparison theorems – the deepest part of the
p
-adic comparison theorems
via syntomic cohomology of
[
20
,
14
,
21
,
22
]
(or their versions using cohomology of period sheaves
of
[
10
]
) –
for dagger varieties and properties of their Hyodo-Kato cohomology (and filtered
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology) to control the functional analytic properties of pro-étale cohomology sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve to be able to apply fully-faithfulness results (akin to the arguments sketched in Section
1.3
).

Finally, we would like to mention a different approach to duality theorems developed recently by Shizhang Li, Reinecke, and Zavyalov (see
[
26
]
), which works for proper smooth rigid analytic varieties (and their relative incarnations) and, after some modifications, can be transferred to the Fargues-Fontaine curve yielding, after descending to VS’s, a version of duality allowing
{\bf Q}_{p}
-local systems (see
[
27
]
).

Notation and conventions.

Let
p
be a prime and let
K
be a complete discrete valuation field with a perfect residue field, of mixed characteristic.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
be the ring of integers in
K
, and
k
be its
residue field.
Let
W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors of
k
and let
F
be its
fraction field (i.e.,
W(k)={\mathcal{O}}_{F}
).

Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
and let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{K}}
denote the integral closure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
in
\overline{K}
. Let
C=\widehat{\overline{K}}
be the
p
-adic completion of
\overline{K}
.
Set
{\mathcal{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
. Let
\breve{C}={\rm Frac}(W(\overline{k}))
.

We will denote by
{\bf B}_{\operatorname{cr}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{st}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}
the crystalline, semistable, and de Rham period rings of Fontaine.

All rigid analytic spaces and dagger spaces considered will be over
K
or
C
; we assume that they are separated, taut, and countable at infinity. Huber pairs will always be sheafy. The category of affinoid perfectoid spaces over an affinoid perfectoid space
S
over
C
will be denoted
by
{\rm Perf}_{S}
.

We will use condensed mathematics as developed in
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
.
We fix an implicit cut-off cardinal
\kappa
(in the sense of
[
31
, Sec. 4]
),
and assume all our perfectoid spaces, and condensed sets to be
\kappa
-small.

If
L={\bf Q}_{p},K,C
, we will denote by
C_{L}
the category of locally convex topological vector spaces over
L
.

We will use the bracket notation for certain limits:

[C_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}C_{2}]
denotes the mapping fiber of
f
and we set

\left[\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.
Quasi-coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve

Here, we will review briefly basic facts concerning quasi-coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve. This is partly based on
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
, and
[
10
, Sec. 6.2]
.

2.1.
Fargues-Fontaine curve

Recall the definition of the relative Fargues-Fontaine curve (see
[
32
, Lecture 12]
). Let
S={\rm Spa}(R,R^{+})
be an affinoid perfectoid space over the finite field
{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
. Let

Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}:={\rm Spa}(W(R^{+}),W(R^{+}))\mathbin{\fgebackslash}V(p[p^{\flat}])

be the relative mixed characteristic punctured unit disc.
It is an analytic adic space over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. The
Frobenius on
R^{+}
induces the Witt vector Frobenius and hence a Frobenius
\varphi
on
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
with free and
totally discontinuous action. The Fargues-Fontaine curve relative to
S

(and
{\bf Q}_{p}
) is defined as

X_{\operatorname{FF},S}:=Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}/\varphi^{{\bf Z}}.

For an interval
I=[s,r]\subset(0,\infty)
with rational endpoints, we have the open subset

Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{I}:=\{|\cdot|:|p|^{r}\leq|[p^{\flat}]|\leq|p|^{s}\}\subset Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}.

It is a rational open subset of
{\rm Spa}(W(R^{+}),W(R^{+}))
hence
an affinoid space,

Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{I}:={\rm Spa}({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S}).

One can form
X_{\operatorname{FF},S}
as the quotient of
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[1,p]}
via the identification

\varphi:Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[1,1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[p,p]}
.
If
S={\rm Spa}(C^{\flat},{\mathcal{O}}_{C^{\flat}})
, we will write
Y_{\operatorname{FF}},X_{\operatorname{FF}},Y_{\operatorname{FF}}^{I},{\bf B}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}
.

We will denote by
x_{\infty}
the
(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})
-point of the curve
X_{\operatorname{FF}}
corresponding to Fontaine’s map

\theta:W({\mathcal{O}}_{C})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
, by
y_{\infty}
– the corresponding point on
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
, and by

\iota_{\infty}:{\rm Spa}(C,{\mathcal{O}}_{C})\to T_{\operatorname{FF}}
,
T=X,Y
, the corresponding closed immersions. More generally, if
S
is the tilt of a perfectoid
space
S^{\sharp}
over
{\rm Spa}({\bf Q}_{p})
, there is an induced closed immersion
\theta:S^{\sharp}\hookrightarrow Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
which
is locally given by Fontaine’s map
\theta:W(R^{+})\to R^{\sharp,+}
. We will denote by
\iota_{\infty}:S^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\to}}T_{FF,S}
the induced
closed immersions and by
y_{\infty},x_{\infty}
, the corresponding divisors.

We set

{\bf B}_{S}:=\lim_{I\subset(0,\infty)}{\bf B}_{S}^{I},

where
I
varies over all the compact intervals of
(0,\infty)
with rational endpoints. We will denote by

{\bf B}_{S,\log}
the log-crystalline period ring (see
[
24
, Sec. 10.3.1]
). We have
{\bf B}_{S}[U]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\bf B}_{S,\log},

U\mapsto\log([p^{\flat}]/p)
, with

\varphi(U)=pU,\sigma(U)=U+\log[\sigma(p^{\flat})/p^{\flat}]
, for
\sigma\in{\mathcal{G}}_{K}
, and
N=-d/dU
. We define
{\bf B}_{S,\log}^{I}
in a similar manner.

2.2.
Quasi-coherent sheaves on the Farges-Fontaine curve

We will present now quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{\operatorname{FF}}
as
\varphi
-modules on a convenient chart of
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
.

2.2.1.
Solid quasi-coherent sheaves

We start with a brief survey of solid quasi-coherent sheaves.
Let
Y
be an analytic adic space over
{\bf Q}_{p}
. We denote by
{\rm QCoh}(Y)
the
\infty
-category of solid quasi-coherent sheaves
on
Y
, and by
{\rm Nuc}(Y)
the full
\infty
-subcategory of solid nuclear sheaves on
Y
.
See
[
2
]
,
[
3
]
for the definitions of these categories and their basic properties.
We will often drop the word "solid" if this does not cause confusion.
If
Y={\rm Spa}(R,R^{+})
, then we have an equivalence
[
2
, Th. 1.6]

{\rm QCoh}(Y)\simeq{\mathcal{D}}((R,R^{+})_{\Box}),

(2.1)

where the latter is the derived category of solid
(R,R^{+})
-modules, i.e., modules over the analytic ring
(R,R^{+})_{\Box}
. In what follows, if this does not confusion,
we will write

R_{\rm an}:=(R,R^{+})_{\Box}

For a general
Y
, the category
{\rm QCoh}(Y)
is obtained by gluing the categories
{\mathcal{D}}((R,R^{+})_{\Box})

in the analytic topology.

By
{\rm Perf}(Y)
, we denote

the full
\infty
-subcategory of perfect sheaves on
Y
;
that is, complexes which locally for the analytic topology are quasi-isomorphic to a bounded complex of finite, locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
-modules. If
Y={\rm Spa}(R,R^{+})
is affinoid, then the natural functor

{\rm Perf}(R)\to{\rm Perf}(Y)

is an equivalence, where the left-hand side denotes the
\infty
-category of perfect complexes of
R
-modules (i.e., bounded complexes of finite projective
R
-modules).

The categories
{\rm QCoh}(Y)
,
{\rm Nuc}(Y)
, and
{\rm Perf}(Y)
are (compatibly) symmetric monoidal. In the definition of the
\infty
-category
{\rm QCoh}(Y)
we will bound everything by a fixed uncountable cardinal so that the category is presentable; it is then also
closed symmetric monoidal. The
\infty
-category
{\rm Nuc}(Y)
is as well presentable and closed symmetric monoidal. Similarly for the
\infty
-category

{\rm Perf}(Y)
.

Remark 2.2
.

The categories
{\rm QCoh}(Y)
,
{\rm Nuc}(Y)
, and
{\rm Perf}(Y)
can be defined in a more general setting, where
Y={\rm Spa}(R,R^{+})
is a pair such that
R
is a complete Huber ring and
R^{+}\subset R^{0}
is an arbitrary subring (see
[
2
, Sec. 3.3]
for details). We will most often use the case when
R^{+}={\bf Z}
.

2.2.2.
Quasi-coherent
\varphi
-sheaves on
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}

The
\infty
-category of quasi-coherent
\varphi
-equivariant sheaves over
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
(in short:
\varphi
-sheaves over
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
) is the equalizer

{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}:={\rm eq}\big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.86884pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.86884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.21603pt\raise-8.2758pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\rm Id}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.14867pt\raise 9.1979pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big).

It is the
\infty
-category of pairs
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi_{{\mathcal{E}}})
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
and

\varphi_{{\mathcal{E}}}:\varphi^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathcal{E}}
is a quasi-isomorphism
7
7

7

We will call isomorphisms in the
\infty
-categories
{\rm QCoh}(-)
quasi-isomorphisms to be compatible with more classical set-ups.
.
The category
{\rm Nuc}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
(resp.
{\rm Perf}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi})
is the full
\infty
-subcategory of
{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
spanned by
the pairs
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi_{{\mathcal{E}}})
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a nuclear (resp. perfect) sheaf on
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
.

In what follows we will set

u=(p-1)/p
,
v=p-1
if
p\neq 2
;

for
p=2
we take
u=3/4
,
v=3/2
.

If
S
is the tilt of a perfectoid space
S^{\natural}
over
{\rm Spa}({\bf Q}_{p})
,
this choice of
u,v
ensures that the divisor on
Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u,v]}
associated to
t

is
y_{\infty}
and
t
is a unit in
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]}
, i.e., if

S^{\natural}={\rm Spa}(R,R^{+})
, then
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]}/t=R
and
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]}/t=0
.

Via analytic descent,
we like to describe the above categories of
\varphi
-equivariant sheaves using the chart
Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u,v]}
(via Frobenius we have
\varphi:Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u/p,v/p]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u,v]}
):

{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}\simeq{\rm eq}\big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.86884pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.86884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[u,v]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.92177pt\raise-9.19789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.67717pt\raise 9.1979pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.86884pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[u,v/p]}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)\big).

We wrote here
\varphi,j
for the Frobenius and the open embedding maps from
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[u,v/p]}
to
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[u,v]}
, respectively.
That is,
{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
is the
\infty
-category of pairs
({\mathcal{E}},\varphi_{{\mathcal{E}}})
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}^{[u,v]}
and

\varphi_{{\mathcal{E}}}:\varphi^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}j^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. The categories
{\rm Nuc}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
,
{\rm Perf}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
can be described in an analogous way.

We note that, since we have the equivalence (
2.1
), we can also write
8
8

8

We stress here that
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box})
and
{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box}
is just a notation; the ring
{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S}
does not exist.

{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}\simeq{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}:={\rm eq}\big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.93408pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.93408pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.98701pt\raise-9.19789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.24103pt\raise 9.1979pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big).

(Frobenius
\varphi
maps
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}
to
{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}
.) It is the
\infty
-category of pairs

M=(M^{[u,v]},\varphi_{M})
, where
M^{[u,v]}
is a complex of
{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}
-modules and
the Frobenius
\varphi_{M}
is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of
{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}
-modules

\varphi_{M}:\varphi^{*}M^{[u,v]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{[u,v/p]}:=M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}}{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}.

Remark 2.3
.

In what follows it will be convenient
to consider the following variant
{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S})^{\varphi}
(in which we drop the “an”, i.e., we
consider
(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}
instead of
(R,R^{+})_{\Box}
)
of the
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
:

{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S})^{\varphi}:={\rm eq}\big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 22.93408pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.93408pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{[u,v]}_{S,\Box})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.98701pt\raise-9.19789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.24103pt\raise 9.1979pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.93408pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,\Box})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big).

It is the
\infty
-category of pairs
M=(M^{[u,v]},\varphi_{M})
, where
M^{[u,v]}
is a complex of solid
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]}
-modules and
the Frobenius
\varphi_{M}
is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of solid
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]}
-modules

\varphi_{M}:\varphi^{*}M^{[u,v]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{[u,v/p]}:=M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}}{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]}.

We call
{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S})^{\varphi}

the category of
\varphi
-complexes of
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S}
-modules
.
Since we have the equivalences of symmetric monoidal categories

{\mathcal{D}}(({{\bf B}}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box})={\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{I}_{S,{\rm an}})

(see
[
9
, Lemma A.16]
), this corresponds to using the analytic structure with respect to
{\bf Z}
in place of
{{\bf B}}^{I,+}_{S}
.
In particular, we have a canonical monoidal functor
{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S})^{\varphi}\to{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
.

2.2.3.
Monoidal structure on quasi-coherent sheaves on
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}

The category
{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
is closed symmetric monoidal. We will now present how the closed symmetric monoidal structure can be seen on the level of the category
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
. In what follows we have set

{\bf B}_{1}:={\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}
,
{\bf B}_{2}:={\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}
.

The (derived) tensor product in
{{\mathcal{D}}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
,
denoted by
(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box}}(-)
, is inherited from the one of the
category
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}_{1})
. More precisely,
for
(M,\varphi_{M}),(N,\varphi_{N})\in{{\mathcal{D}}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
, their tensor product is defined by:

\displaystyle M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box}}N:=(M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}N^{[u,v]},\varphi_{M\otimes N}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{M\otimes N}=\varphi_{M}\otimes\varphi_{N}:(M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1},\varphi}{\bf B}_{2}\to(M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{{\bf B}_{1}}}{\bf B}_{2}=(M^{[u,v/p]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{2}}N^{[u,v/p]}).

Frobenius
\varphi_{M\otimes N}
is a quasi-isomorphism because so are Frobeniuses
\varphi_{M}
and
\varphi_{N}
.

The internal
{\mathrm{R}}{\rm{Hom}}
, denoted by
{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box}}(-,-)
, in
the category
{{\mathcal{D}}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S,\Box})^{\varphi}
is defined by:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S,\Box}}(M,N):=({\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]}),\varphi_{M,N}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{M,N}:=(\varphi_{M}^{-1},\varphi_{N}):\quad{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1},\varphi}{\bf B}_{2}\to{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}.

In the definition of Frobenius
\varphi_{M,N}
we have used the following (non-obvious
9
9

9

One
usually needs some finiteness condition for this kind of statements to hold.
)
fact:

Lemma 2.4
.

The canonical maps

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1},\varphi}{\bf B}_{2}

\displaystyle\to{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{2}}(M^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1},\varphi}{\bf B}_{2},N^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1},\varphi}{\bf B}_{2}),

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}

\displaystyle\to{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{2}}(M^{[u,v/p]},N^{[u,v/p]})

are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

To start, note that, since the first map is induced by the composition of the maps

\varphi:{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{S,{\rm an}}{\to}{\bf B}^{[u/p,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}},\quad\operatorname{can}:{\bf B}^{[u/p,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}\to{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{S,{\rm an}}

where the first map is an isomorphism, it suffices to argue for the second quasi-isomorphism in the lemma.

Write
M^{[u,v]}=\operatorname{colim}_{i\in I}M_{i}^{[u,v]}
as a colimit of compact
projective objects
\{M_{i}^{[u,v]}={\bf B}_{1}[T_{i}]\}
,
i\in I
, for extremally disconnected sets
T_{i}
’s. Then

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(\operatorname{colim}_{i\in I}M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})

\displaystyle\simeq{\rm Rlim}_{I}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})

and similarly for
[u,v/p]
(we set
M_{i}^{[u,v/p]}:=M_{i}^{[u,v]}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}\simeq{\bf B}_{2}[T_{i}]
). It follows that it suffices to show that

({\rm Rlim}_{I}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]}))\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Rlim}_{I}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{2}}(M_{i}^{[u,v/p]},N^{[u,v/p]}).

But, by
[
2
, Prop. 5.38]
, we have

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{2}}(M_{i}^{[u,v/p]},N^{[u,v/p]}).

Hence it suffices to show that

({\rm Rlim}_{I}{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]}))\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Rlim}_{I}({\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}(M_{i}^{[u,v]},N^{[u,v]})\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}).

That is, that the functor
(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}
commutes with derived limits.

To show this write
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]}={\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]}\langle f\rangle
, where
f=(p/[p^{\flat}]^{p/v})\in{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]}
.
By
[
2
, Prop. 4.11]
, we have

\displaystyle(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}\simeq(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z})_{\Box}}({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z}[T])_{\Box},

(2.5)

where the map
({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z})_{\Box}\to({\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]},{\bf B}^{[u,v],+}_{S})_{\Box}

is induced by
T\mapsto f
. But, by
[
2
, Prop. 3.12]
, for
M\in{\mathcal{D}}(({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z})_{\Box})
, we have

M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z})_{\Box}}({\bf Z}[T],{\bf Z}[T])_{\Box}\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{R}(R_{\infty}/R,M)[1],

where
R={\bf Z}[T]
,
R_{\infty}={\bf Z}((T^{-1}))
. It follows that the functor
(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}_{1}}{\bf B}_{2}
commutes with derived limits, as wanted.
∎

Finally, we note that Frobenius
\varphi_{M,N}
is a quasi-isomorphism because so are Frobeniuses
\varphi_{M}
and
\varphi_{N}
.

Remark 2.6
.

(1) The tensor product computations above are valid for the category
{\mathcal{D}}({{\bf B}}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S})^{\varphi}
. For the internal
{\rm{Hom}}
, they go through as well if one assumes that
(M,\varphi_{M}),(N,\varphi_{N})
are nuclear and so is the internal
{\rm{Hom}}
between them (see
[
28
, Lemma 4.7]
for a proof of an analog of Lemma
2.4
in this setting). In this paper we will always be in this setting.

(2) Let
M,N\in{\mathcal{D}}((R,{\bf Z})_{\Box})
. We note that the natural map

{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}}(M,N)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}}(R,R^{+})_{\Box}\to{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{(R,R^{+})_{\Box}}(M\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}}(R,R^{+})_{\Box},N\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}}(R,R^{+})_{\Box})

is a quasi-isomorphism in the case
N
is
(R,R^{+})_{\Box}
-complete. It follows that
{\mathrm{R}}\underline{{\rm{Hom}}}_{(R,{\bf Z})_{\Box}}(M,N)
is then also
(R,R^{+})_{\Box}
-complete. For example, this is the case when
N
is nuclear.

2.2.4.
Quasi-coherent
\varphi
-sheaves on
Y_{\operatorname{FF}}
and
\varphi
-modules

We will now describe the categories
{\rm Nuc}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
and
{\rm Perf}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}
using complexes of (usual) solid modules.

Recall that the natural maps of analytic rings

({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box}\to({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S})_{\Box}

induce base change
functors

(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box}}({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S})_{\Box}:\quad{\mathcal{D}}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box})\to{\mathcal{D}}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S})_{\Box}).

(2.7)

By
[
10
, (6.13)]
, the functors (
2.7
) induce equivalences on the full subcategories of nuclear and perfect complexes:

\displaystyle{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}_{S}^{I}):={\rm Nuc}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Nuc}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S})_{\Box}),

(2.8)

\displaystyle{\rm Perf}({\bf B}_{S}^{I})\simeq{\rm Perf}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf Z})_{\Box})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Perf}(({\bf B}_{S}^{I},{\bf B}^{I,+}_{S})_{\Box}).

We define the category
{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
(resp.
{\rm Perf}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi})
as the full
\infty
-subcategory of
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
spanned by
the pairs
(M^{[u,v]},\varphi_{M})
, where
M^{[u,v]}
is a nuclear (resp. perfect)
complex over
{\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]}
.
That is, the
\infty
-category
{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
of nuclear
\varphi
-complexes of
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S}
-modules, is defined as the equalizer:

{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}:={\rm eq}\big(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.86464pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-27.86464pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.88545pt\raise-7.3522pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{can}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.86464pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.17159pt\raise 9.1979pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varphi^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.86464pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.86464pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v/p]})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\big).

Similarly, for the category
{\rm Perf}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
of
\varphi
-complexes of perfect
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S}
-modules.

We have the following simple fact:

Lemma 2.9
.

The canonical functor

{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}\to{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})

induces equivalences of
\infty
-categories:

{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Nuc}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi},\quad{\rm Perf}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Perf}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}.

(2.10)

Proof.

Our claim follows from equivalences (
2.8
).
∎

The categories
{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
, and
{\rm Perf}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
are symmetric monoidal: the (derived) tensor products
(denoted by
(-)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}}_{{\bf B}^{\operatorname{FF}}_{S}}(-)
) are inherited from the ones of the
categories
{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]})
, and
{\rm Perf}({\bf B}_{S}^{[u,v]})
, respectively. The canonical functor to the category
{{\mathcal{D}}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}
is symmetric monoidal.
The functors in Lemma
2.9
are compatible with these structures.

2.2.5.
Quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{\operatorname{FF}}

The action of
\varphi
on
Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}
being free and totally discontinuous, by the analytic descent for solid quasi-coherent sheaves,
we obtain an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF},S}:\quad{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm QCoh}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S}/\varphi^{{\bf Z}})={\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}).

Similarly, we get equivalences of closed symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories

{\rm Nuc}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Nuc}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}),\quad{\rm Perf}(Y_{\operatorname{FF},S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Perf}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}).

(2.11)

By Lemma
2.9
, this yields a functor

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF},S}:{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}\to{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}).

(2.12)

We will often skip the subscript
S
from
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF},S}
if this does not cause confusion. Restricting to nuclear or perfect complexes we get the following result (see
[
10
, Th. 6.8]
for a similar statement):

Proposition 2.13
.

(1)
The functor
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF},S}
, from (
2.12
), induces equivalences of
\infty
-categories

{\rm Nuc}({\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Nuc}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}),\quad{\rm Perf}({\bf B}_{S})^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm Perf}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S}).

(2.14)

(2)
Let
{\mathcal{E}}\in{\rm Nuc}(X_{\operatorname{FF},S})
. Let
(M({\mathcal{E}})^{[u,v]},\varphi_{M})
be the nuclear
\varphi
-complex of
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}_{S}
-modules
corresponding to
{\mathcal{E}}
via (
2.14
). Then, there
is a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf Q}_{p}(S)_{\Box})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{FF},S},{\mathcal{E}})\simeq[M({\mathcal{E}})^{[u,v]}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\varphi-1}}M({\mathcal{E}})^{[u,v/p]}].

Proof.

The first claim is a combination of (
2.10
) and (
2.11
).
For the second claim, we compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{FF},S},{\mathcal{E}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\varphi^{{\bf Z}},{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(Y_{\rm FF},{\mathcal{E}}_{|Y_{\rm FF}}))\simeq[\Gamma(Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u,v]},{\mathcal{E}}_{|Y_{\rm FF}})\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\varphi-1}}\Gamma(Y_{{\rm FF},S}^{[u,v/p]},{\mathcal{E}}_{|Y_{\rm FF}})]

\displaystyle\simeq[M({\mathcal{E}})^{[u,v]}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\varphi-1}}M({\mathcal{E}})^{[u,v/p]}].

∎

3.
Syntomic complexes on the Fargues-Fontaine curve

In this section we define quasi-coherent sheaves on the Fargues-Fontaine curve
representing various cohomologies of smooth partially proper rigid analytic varieties:
de Rham (Proposition
3.1
), Hyodo-Kato (Proposition
3.6
),
and syntomic (Formula
3.22
) and Proposition
3.23
).
We will do the same for pro-étale cohomology in the next chapter (Proposition
4.3
).

3.1.
de Rham cohomology

We start with the cohomologies of de Rham type.
We use
[
1
, Sec. 3, Sec. 4, Sec. 5]
as the basic reference.

3.1.1.
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology

Let
X
be a partially proper rigid analytic variety over
K
. We have the (filtered) de Rham complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}(K_{\Box})
and (filtered)
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box})
, respectively:

F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X),\quad F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C}/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}),\quad r\in{\bf N},\ ?=-,c

as well as the quotients

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{r}.

The latter complexes can be represented by quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{\rm FF}
. For
r\in{\bf N}
, we define the
de Rham modules

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{[u,v]}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r).

Since
{\bf B}_{[u,v]}/t^{i}={\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{i}
, these are
{\bf B}_{[u,v]}
-modules. They are nuclear: for the usual cohomology, in the Stein case this follows from Section
3.1.2
below; in general case – by the fact that nuclearity is preserved by countable products. For the cohomology with compact support: we use the Stein case again and then pass to a colimit which preserves nuclearity. Since
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{[u,v]}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{{\bf B}_{[u,v]}}{\bf B}_{[u,v/p]}=0
(recall that
t
is invertible in
{\bf B}_{[u,v/p]}
),
these complexes taken as pairs

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r)=({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{[u,v]}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r),0)

define nuclear
\varphi
-complexes over
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}

(see Remark
2.3
).

We denote by

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r):={\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r))

the corresponding nuclear quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{\operatorname{FF}}
. We will call them
de Rham sheaves
. We record the following simple fact:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
r\in{\bf N}
. We have a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\rm QCoh}(X_{\operatorname{FF}})

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r)\simeq i_{\infty,*}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{C},r).

For
S\in{\rm Perf}_{C}
,
by replacing
{\bf B},{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}},X_{\operatorname{FF}}
with
{\bf B}_{S^{\flat}},{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}(S),X_{\operatorname{FF},S^{\flat}}
in the above, we obtain de Rham modules and sheaves on
X_{\operatorname{FF},S^{\flat}}
:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{S},r),{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{dR},?}(X_{S},r)
. These are functors on
{\rm Perf}_{C}
.

3.1.2.
Stein varieties

Let
X
be a smooth Stein rigid analytic variety over
K
. In this case the above cohomology complexes can be made more explicit.

(
\bullet
)
De Rham cohomology.
Let
r\in{\bf N}
. Since coherent cohomology of
X
is trivial in nonzero degrees and we have Serre duality, the (filtered) de Rham cohomology of
X
can be computed by the following complexes
in
{\mathcal{D}}(K_{\Box})
:

\displaystyle F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)

\displaystyle\simeq(\Omega^{r}(X)\to\cdots\to\Omega^{d}(X))[-r],

\displaystyle F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X)

\displaystyle\simeq(H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{r})\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{r+1})\to\cdots\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{d}))[-d-r].

The second quasi-isomorphism follows from the fact that
H^{i}_{c}(X,\Omega^{j})=0
, for
i\neq d
. The terms of the first complex are nuclear Fréchet over
K

and those of the second complex are of compact type over
K
(in classical terminology).

(
\bullet
)
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology.
Let
r\in{\bf N}
. The (filtered)
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology of
X
can be computed by the following complexes in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box})
:

\displaystyle F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C}/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to\Omega^{1}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r-1}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to\cdots\to\Omega^{d}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r-d}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}},

(3.2)

\displaystyle F^{r}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X_{C}/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})

\displaystyle\simeq(H^{d}_{c}(X,{\mathcal{O}})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{1})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r-1}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to\cdots\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{d})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}t^{r-d}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})[-d].

The tensor products are actually derived because
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
is Fréchet hence flat.

This yields the quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box})
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C},r)

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathcal{O}}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r})\to\Omega^{1}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r-1})\to\cdots\to\Omega^{d}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r-d}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X_{C},r)

\displaystyle\simeq(H^{d}_{c}(X,{\mathcal{O}})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r})\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{1})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r-1})\to\cdots\to H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{d})\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/t^{r-d}))[-d].

We will denote the respective cohomology groups by
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X,r)
and
H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X,r)
.

For
i\geq 0
, we have short exact sequences in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box})
(see
[
14
, Example 3.30]
,
[
1
, Lemma 3.14]
)

\displaystyle 0\to\Omega^{i}(X_{C})/{\rm Im}\,d\to

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X_{C},r)\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR}}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{r-i-1})\to 0,

(3.4)

\displaystyle 0\to(H^{d}_{c}(X,\Omega^{i-d})/{\rm Im}\,d)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}{\rm gr}^{r-i+d-1}_{F}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\to

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X_{C},r)\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{K}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}/F^{r-i+d-1})\to 0.

3.2.
Hyodo-Kato cohomology

Let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic variety over
C
.
Let
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\in{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi,N,{\mathcal{G}}_{K}}(\breve{C}_{\Box})
be the Hyodo-Kato cohomology defined in
[
21
, Sec. 4]
(see also
[
10
, Sec. 3]
).
Here
{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi,N,{\mathcal{G}}_{K}}(\breve{C}_{\Box})
is the derived
\infty
-category of solid
(\varphi,N,{\mathcal{G}}_{K})
-modules over
\breve{C}
.

3.2.1.
Hyodo-Kato cohomology on the Fargues-Fontaine curve

Let
r\in{\bf Z}
. Consider the twisted Hyodo-Kato cohomology in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi,{\mathcal{G}}_{K}}(\breve{C}_{\Box})

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{I}(X_{C},r):=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}^{I}_{\log}]^{N=0},

where the twist
\{r\}
means Frobenius divided by
p^{r}
and
I\subset(0,\infty)
is a compact interval with rational endpoints. We define
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{\bf B}}(X_{C},r)
in a similar way. We claim that, for compact intervals
I\subset J\subset(0,\infty)
with rational endpoints, we have the canonical quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{J}(X_{C},r)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{{\bf B}^{J}}{\bf B}^{I}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{I}(X_{C},r).

Indeed, for that, it suffices to show that the canonical map

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}^{J}_{\log}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{{\bf B}^{J}}{\bf B}^{I}\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}^{I}_{\log}

is a quasi-isomorphism. But this is clear since the solid tensor product commutes with direct sums.

We define the pair
10
10

10

There is a certain doubling of notation with the previous paragraph but we hope that this will not cause confusion in what follows.

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r):=({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{[u,v]}}(X_{C},r),\varphi),\quad\varphi:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{[u,v]}}(X_{C},r)\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{[u,v/p]}}(X_{C},r),

where the Frobenius
\varphi
is induced from the Hyodo-Kato Frobenius and the Frobenius
\varphi:{\bf B}^{[u,v]}\to{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}
. It
yields a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}_{\Box})

\varphi:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{[u,v]}}(X_{C},r)\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{{\bf B}^{[u,v]},\varphi}{\bf B}^{[u,v/p]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}^{{[u,v/p]}}(X_{C},r).

The pair
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r)

defines a nuclear
\varphi
-complex (actually
(\varphi,{\mathcal{G}}_{K})
-complex) over
{\bf B}^{\rm FF}
, which we will call
Hyodo-Kato module
.

We define
Hyodo-Kato sheaves
on
X_{\rm FF}
as

{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r):={\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{FF}}({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{{\bf B}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r)).

By Proposition
2.13
, these are nuclear quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{\operatorname{FF}}
. If the cohomology groups of
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})
are of finite rank over
\breve{C}
then the sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r)
is perfect.
By Proposition
2.13
and
[
10
, Th. 6.3]
, we have natural quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{FF}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C},r))

\displaystyle\simeq[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{\log}]^{N=0,\varphi=1}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}_{\log}]^{N=0,\varphi=1}

where we set, for
M={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X_{C})\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}^{[u,v]}_{\log}
or

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\{r\}\otimes^{\operatorname{L}_{\Box}}_{\breve{C}}{\bf B}_{\log}
,

[M]^{N=0,\varphi=1}:=\left[\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern
